========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hello there, doc. I, uh... I guess I should just start by saying I wasn't entirely sure about coming in today. You get to be my age, seventy-four years old, and you just sort of figure things are gonna break down or not work the way they used to, you know? But... well, mostly I'm here because I just don't sleep right anymore. It's been driving me absolutely crazy. 

I go to bed, I got my room all set up, nice and quiet, but I just lie there. I lie there for a really long time, tossing and turning, just staring up at the ceiling and watching the shadows from the streetlamp outside. And then, if I do finally manage to drift off, it ain't a peaceful rest. I end up waking up from these bad dreams, or sometimes it's just... little noises. Like a floorboard creaking, or the wind hitting the window, or a car driving by a block over. The tiniest little sound and my eyes just pop wide open. 

And the worst part is, once I'm awake like that, I can't seem to settle back down. I try getting up, sitting in my recliner in the living room, maybe having a glass of water, but I just can't get back to sleep no matter what I do. Night after night, it's just... it wears a fella out, not getting any proper rest.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, I wouldn't say that. I ain't really a worrier, never have been. I mean, I ain't sitting up in bed fretting about the electric bill or if the roof is gonna leak or nothing like that. My mind ain't racing at all. 

Usually, I just watch a little television in the evening—maybe the local news or one of them old Westerns on the classic channel—and I feel right calm, you know? I turn off the set, go in the bathroom to wash my face, brush my teeth... still got most of my own teeth, which ain't too bad for a fella my age... and I get into bed. I get under the covers and my head is just plain quiet. I ain't anxious. I might lay there and think about, oh, I don't know, what I'm gonna make for breakfast the next morning, or if I ought to sweep the front porch, just regular everyday stuff. 

It ain't racing. It's just... wide awake, if you catch my drift. It's like my brain just don't know it's supposed to shut off. So I just lay there, staring at that ceiling, waiting for a sleep that just won't come. And then, like I was saying before, that's when I start hearing every little creak and groan of the house...

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, early... see, I don't know about early, exactly. It ain't like I'm waking up at four in the morning when the roosters crow, not that we got roosters around my neighborhood anymore. Old man Miller down the street used to have a few chickens in his backyard, oh, maybe twenty or twenty-five years ago, but the city made him get rid of 'em because of the noise. Caused a whole big fuss, people complaining and all that. 

Anyway, no, I wouldn't say I wake up earlier than I want to in the morning. My regular time to get out of bed has always been about six-thirty, maybe seven. Even back when I was working, doing manual labor, I'd be up by six, so my body is just plain used to that. If I actually make it to the morning, I just get up at my regular time, put my slippers on, and head to the kitchen to get the coffee going. 

The trouble, see, like I was trying to tell you before, is the middle of the night. It ain't the morning that's the problem. It's the two in the morning, three in the morning wake-ups. That's when I pop awake from a bad dream or a floorboard groaning, and then I'm just stuck. I'll go sit in my recliner in the living room, maybe look out the front window at that streetlamp I mentioned, just watching the moths flying around the light. I just sit there for hours, rubbing my knees, wishing I was asleep. But as for the actual morning time? No, I don't wake up too early then. If I'm lucky enough to be asleep when the sun starts coming up, I'll just sleep right till seven like I always have.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, you'd think I would be, wouldn't you? What with all that sitting up in the dark and pacing around the living room. But honestly... no, I ain't really sleepy during the daytime. It's the strangest thing. 

I mean, you take my brother-in-law, Frank, for example. He's a year younger than me, seventy-three, and he sleeps like a log all night, but come two in the afternoon, he's dozing off in his armchair. Can't even make it through a whole episode of the game shows without his chin hitting his chest and him snoring away. But me? I've never been much of a napper. Never really had the time for it back when I was working, so I guess I just never got the habit. 

Even with the bad sleep at night now, once the sun is up and I get that first cup of coffee in me, I'm just... I'm awake. I might be a little stiff, you know, my knees and my back ain't exactly what they used to be from all those years hauling things around the work site, but I ain't yawning or feeling like my eyes are heavy. I just go about my day. I'll read the morning paper, maybe go out and tinker a bit in the garage—I got this old push mower I've been meaning to fix up, just needs a little cleaning out, I figure—and I just keep myself moving. So, no, doc, I don't really get tired or sleepy when the sun's out. It's just when the sun goes down that the trouble starts.

Well, no... no, I can't say that I do. My legs... well, like I said, my knees ache a fair bit, especially when it's damp out or if I've been standing on the concrete floor in the garage too long. That concrete is mighty hard on the joints, you know. I used to wear these thick work boots with the extra padding, but nowadays I just wear my old sneakers, and they don't give much support. 

But as for an uncomfortable feeling... like an urge to move 'em? No, nothing like that. When I'm sitting in my recliner in the middle of the night, my legs are perfectly still. I mean, I might rub my knees, like I told you, just to work the stiffness out, or maybe I'll cross one ankle over the other, but they ain't twitching or restless or nothing. They just sit there like the rest of me, wide awake and waiting for the morning. 

I did know a fella once, guy named Earl I worked with down at the lumber yard... oh, must be thirty years ago now. He used to say his legs felt like they had bugs crawling inside 'em at night. Said he had to get up and walk around the block at two in the morning just to get 'em to settle down. His wife thought he was crazy, going out for a stroll in his pajamas. But me? No, my legs are fine, just a bit stiff from the years. It's just my brain that won't let me sleep, not my legs.

I might lay there and think about, oh, I don't know, what I'm gonna make for breakfast the next morning, or if I ought to sweep the front porch, just regular everyday stuff. \n\nIt ain't racing. It's just... wide awake, if you catch my drift. It's like my brain just don't know it's supposed to shut off. So I just lay there, staring at that ceiling, waiting for a sleep that just won't come. And then, like I was saying before, that's when I start hearing every little creak and groan of the house...\n\nDoctor: Do you usually wake up earlier than you'd like to, and if so, do you find it hard to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, early... see, I don't know about early, exactly. It ain't like I'm waking up at four in the morning when the roosters crow, not that we got roosters around my neighborhood anymore. Old man Miller down the street used to have a few chickens in his backyard, oh, maybe twenty or twenty-five years ago, but the city made him get rid of 'em because of the noise. Caused a whole big fuss, people complaining and all that. \n\nAnyway, no, I wouldn't say I wake up earlier than I want to in the morning. My regular time to get out of bed has always been about six-thirty, maybe seven. Even back when I was working, doing manual labor, I'd be up by six, so my body is just plain used to that. If I actually make it to the morning, I just get up at my regular time, put my slippers on, and head to the kitchen to get the coffee going. \n\nThe trouble, see, like I was trying to tell you before, is the middle of the night. It ain't the morning that's the problem. It's the two in the morning, three in the morning wake-ups. That's when I pop awake from a bad dream or a floorboard groaning, and then I'm just stuck. I'll go sit in my recliner in the living room, maybe look out the front window at that streetlamp I mentioned, just watching the moths flying around the light. I just sit there for hours, rubbing my knees, wishing I was asleep. But as for the actual morning time? No, I don't wake up too early then. If I'm lucky enough to be asleep when the sun starts coming up, I'll just sleep right till seven like I always have.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling tired or sleepy during the day because of these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: Well, you'd think I would be, wouldn't you? What with all that sitting up in the dark and pacing around the living room. But honestly... no, I ain't really sleepy during the daytime. It's the strangest thing. \n\nI mean, you take my brother-in-law, Frank, for example. He's a year younger than me, seventy-three, and he sleeps like a log all night, but come two in the afternoon, he's dozing off in his armchair. Can't even make it through a whole episode of the game shows without his chin hitting his chest and him snoring away. But me? I've never been much of a napper. Never really had the time for it back when I was working, so I guess I just never got the habit. \n\nEven with the bad sleep at night now, once the sun is up and I get that first cup of coffee in me, I'm just... I'm awake. I might be a little stiff, you know, my knees and my back ain't exactly what they used to be from all those years hauling things around the work site, but I ain't yawning or feeling like my eyes are heavy. I just go about my day. I'll read the morning paper, maybe go out and tinker a bit in the garage—I got this old push mower I've been meaning to fix up, just needs a little cleaning out, I figure—and I just keep myself moving. So, no, doc, I don't really get tired or sleepy when the sun's out. It's just when the sun goes down that the trouble starts.\n\nDoctor: When you're lying in bed or sitting quietly at night, do you ever feel an uncomfortable sensation in your legs or a strong urge to move them?\n\nPatient: Well, no... no, I can't say that I do. My legs... well, like I said, my knees ache a fair bit, especially when it's damp out or if I've been standing on the concrete floor in the garage too long. That concrete is mighty hard on the joints, you know. I used to wear these thick work boots with the extra padding, but nowadays I just wear my old sneakers, and they don't give much support. \n\nBut as for an uncomfortable feeling... like an urge to move 'em? No, nothing like that. When I'm sitting in my recliner in the middle of the night, my legs are perfectly still. I mean, I might rub my knees, like I told you, just to work the stiffness out, or maybe I'll cross one ankle over the other, but they ain't twitching or restless or nothing. They just sit there like the rest of me, wide awake and waiting for the morning. \n\nI did know a fella once, guy named Earl I worked with down at the lumber yard... oh, must be thirty years ago now. He used to say his legs felt like they had bugs crawling inside 'em at night. Said he had to get up and walk around the block at two in the morning just to get 'em to settle down. His wife thought he was crazy, going out for a stroll in his pajamas. But me? No, my legs are fine, just a bit stiff from the years. It's just my brain that won't let me sleep, not my legs.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

  "note": "",
  "is_final": false

It's just my brain that won't let me sleep, not my legs.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you snore loudly or have anyone ever told you that you stop breathing or gasp for air during the night?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 74 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Snoring? Well, now... see, since my Mary passed—Lord, it's been six years this November—I've been sleeping alone, mostly. So there ain't nobody right there next to me to complain about it if I do. But even back when she was alive, she never said much about it. Now, my brother-in-law Frank, the one I was telling you about who falls asleep watching the game shows? Oh, he snores like a freight train. You can hear him clear on the other side of the house. His wife, my sister Shirley, she actually makes him sleep in the guest room sometimes when his allergies act up because it gets so loud it rattles the picture frames on the nightstand. 

But me? No, Mary never said I snored loud or nothing. She was a light sleeper, too. If the wind blew too hard against the siding, she'd wake up. But she never poked me in the ribs and told me to roll over or said I was making a racket. And as for stopping breathing or gasping... well, I don't think so. I mean, I'm awake half the night anyway, just sitting there in the dark watching the moths, so I think I'd notice if I suddenly couldn't catch my breath, right? I ain't ever woken up choking or gasping for air. The only thing that wakes me up in a panic is... well, the bad dreams, like I said. But it ain't my breathing that's the problem. It's just my mind running away with me. So, no, doc, I don't think I got any of that going on.

You get to be my age, seventy-four years old, and you just sort of figure things are gonna break down or not work the way they used to, you know? But... well, mostly I'm here because I just don't sleep right anymore. It's been driving me absolutely crazy. \n\nI go to bed, I got my room all set up, nice and quiet, but I just lie there. I lie there for a really long time, tossing and turning, just staring up at the ceiling and watching the shadows from the streetlamp outside. And then, if I do finally manage to drift off, it ain't a peaceful rest. I end up waking up from these bad dreams, or sometimes it's just... little noises. Like a floorboard creaking, or the wind hitting the window, or a car driving by a block over. The tiniest little sound and my eyes just pop wide open. \n\nAnd the worst part is, once I'm awake like that, I can't seem to settle back down. I try getting up, sitting in my recliner in the living room, maybe having a glass of water, but I just can't get back to sleep no matter what I do. Night after night, it's just... it wears a fella out, not getting any proper rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried around bedtime, or does your mind often race with thoughts when you're trying to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that. I ain't really a worrier, never have been. I mean, I ain't sitting up in bed fretting about the electric bill or if the roof is gonna leak or nothing like that. My mind ain't racing at all. \n\nUsually, I just watch a little television in the evening—maybe the local news or one of them old Westerns on the classic channel—and I feel right calm, you know? I turn off the set, go in the bathroom to wash my face, brush my teeth... still got most of my own teeth, which ain't too bad for a fella my age... and I get into bed. I get under the covers and my head is just plain quiet. I ain't anxious. I might lay there and think about, oh, I don't know, what I'm gonna make for breakfast the next morning, or if I ought to sweep the front porch, just regular everyday stuff. \n\nIt ain't racing. It's just... wide awake, if you catch my drift. It's like my brain just don't know it's supposed to shut off. So I just lay there, staring at that ceiling, waiting for a sleep that just won't come. And then, like I was saying before, that's when I start hearing every little creak and groan of the house...\n\nDoctor: Do you usually wake up earlier than you'd like to, and if so, do you find it hard to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, early... see, I don't know about early, exactly. It ain't like I'm waking up at four in the morning when the roosters crow, not that we got roosters around my neighborhood anymore. Old man Miller down the street used to have a few chickens in his backyard, oh, maybe twenty or twenty-five years ago, but the city made him get rid of 'em because of the noise. Caused a whole big fuss, people complaining and all that. \n\nAnyway, no, I wouldn't say I wake up earlier than I want to in the morning. My regular time to get out of bed has always been about six-thirty, maybe seven. Even back when I was working, doing manual labor, I'd be up by six, so my body is just plain used to that. If I actually make it to the morning, I just get up at my regular time, put my slippers on, and head to the kitchen to get the coffee going. \n\nThe trouble, see, like I was trying to tell you before, is the middle of the night. It ain't the morning that's the problem. It's the two in the morning, three in the morning wake-ups. That's when I pop awake from a bad dream or a floorboard groaning, and then I'm just stuck. I'll go sit in my recliner in the living room, maybe look out the front window at that streetlamp I mentioned, just watching the moths flying around the light. I just sit there for hours, rubbing my knees, wishing I was asleep. But as for the actual morning time? No, I don't wake up too early then. If I'm lucky enough to be asleep when the sun starts coming up, I'll just sleep right till seven like I always have.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling tired or sleepy during the day because of these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: Well, you'd think I would be, wouldn't you? What with all that sitting up in the dark and pacing around the living room. But honestly... no, I ain't really sleepy during the daytime. It's the strangest thing. \n\nI mean, you take my brother-in-law, Frank, for example. He's a year younger than me, seventy-three, and he sleeps like a log all night, but come two in the afternoon, he's dozing off in his armchair. Can't even make it through a whole episode of the game shows without his chin hitting his chest and him snoring away. But me? I've never been much of a napper. Never really had the time for it back when I was working, so I guess I just never got the habit. \n\nEven with the bad sleep at night now, once the sun is up and I get that first cup of coffee in me, I'm just... I'm awake. I might be a little stiff, you know, my knees and my back ain't exactly what they used to be from all those years hauling things around the work site, but I ain't yawning or feeling like my eyes are heavy. I just go about my day. I'll read the morning paper, maybe go out and tinker a bit in the garage—I got this old push mower I've been meaning to fix up, just needs a little cleaning out, I figure—and I just keep myself moving. So, no, doc, I don't really get tired or sleepy when the sun's out. It's just when the sun goes down that the trouble starts.\n\nDoctor: When you're lying in bed or sitting quietly at night, do you ever feel an uncomfortable sensation in your legs or a strong urge to move them?\n\nPatient: Well, no... no, I can't say that I do. My legs... well, like I said, my knees ache a fair bit, especially when it's damp out or if I've been standing on the concrete floor in the garage too long. That concrete is mighty hard on the joints, you know. I used to wear these thick work boots with the extra padding, but nowadays I just wear my old sneakers, and they don't give much support. \n\nBut as for an uncomfortable feeling... like an urge to move 'em? No, nothing like that. When I'm sitting in my recliner in the middle of the night, my legs are perfectly still. I mean, I might rub my knees, like I told you, just to work the stiffness out, or maybe I'll cross one ankle over the other, but they ain't twitching or restless or nothing. They just sit there like the rest of me, wide awake and waiting for the morning. \n\nI did know a fella once, guy named Earl I worked with down at the lumber yard... oh, must be thirty years ago now. He used to say his legs felt like they had bugs crawling inside 'em at night. Said he had to get up and walk around the block at two in the morning just to get 'em to settle down. His wife thought he was crazy, going out for a stroll in his pajamas. But me? No, my legs are fine, just a bit stiff from the years. It's just my brain that won't let me sleep, not my legs.\n\nDoctor: Do you snore loudly or have anyone ever told you that you stop breathing or gasp for air during the night?\n\nPatient: Snoring? It's just when the sun goes down that the trouble starts.\n\nDoctor: When you're lying in bed or sitting quietly at night, do you ever feel an uncomfortable sensation in your legs or a strong urge to move them?\n\nPatient: Well, no... no, I can't say that I do. My legs... well, like I said, my knees ache a fair bit, especially when it's damp out or if I've been standing on the concrete floor in the garage too long. That concrete is mighty hard on the joints, you know. I used to wear these thick work boots with the extra padding, but nowadays I just wear my old sneakers, and they don't give much support. \n\nBut as for an uncomfortable feeling... like an urge to move 'em? No, nothing like that. When I'm sitting in my recliner in the middle of the night, my legs are perfectly still. I mean, I might rub my knees, like I told you, just to work the stiffness out, or maybe I'll cross one ankle over the other, but they ain't twitching or restless or nothing. They just sit there like the rest of me, wide awake and waiting for the morning. \n\nI did know a fella once, guy named Earl I worked with down at the lumber yard... oh, must be thirty years ago now. He used to say his legs felt like they had bugs crawling inside 'em at night. Said he had to get up and walk around the block at two in the morning just to get 'em to settle down. His wife thought he was crazy, going out for a stroll in his pajamas. But me? No, my legs are fine, just a bit stiff from the years. It's just my brain that won't let me sleep, not my legs.\n\nDoctor: Do you snore loudly or have anyone ever told you that you stop breathing or gasp for air during the night?\n\nPatient: Snoring? Well, now... see, since my Mary passed—Lord, it's been six years this November—I've been sleeping alone, mostly. So there ain't nobody right there next to me to complain about it if I do. But even back when she was alive, she never said much about it. Now, my brother-in-law Frank, the one I was telling you about who falls asleep watching the game shows? Oh, he snores like a freight train. You can hear him clear on the other side of the house. His wife, my sister Shirley, she actually makes him sleep in the guest room sometimes when his allergies act up because it gets so loud it rattles the picture frames on the nightstand. \n\nBut me? No, Mary never said I snored loud or nothing. She was a light sleeper, too. If the wind blew too hard against the siding, she'd wake up. But she never poked me in the ribs and told me to roll over or said I was making a racket. And as for stopping breathing or gasping... well, I don't think so. I mean, I'm awake half the night anyway, just sitting there in the dark watching the moths, so I think I'd notice if I suddenly couldn't catch my breath, right? I ain't ever woken up choking or gasping for air. The only thing that wakes me up in a panic is... well, the bad dreams, like I said. But it ain't my breathing that's the problem. It's just my mind running away with me. So, no, doc, I don't think I got any of that going on.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow often do these bad dreams happen—nearly every night, a few times a week, or less often?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doc, that's... that's the whole reason I'm sitting here in this chair today, to be honest with you. Mostly I'm here because I just don't sleep right anymore. It ain't just once in a blue moon, neither. It's... well, it feels like it's all the time. I'll get myself ready for bed, you know, wash my face, brush my teeth, check the locks on the doors—always gotta check the locks, maybe twice, just to be sure—and then I'll get under the covers. And I just lie there. For a long, long time. I'll look at the clock, and it'll be eleven, then twelve, then one. Just staring at the ceiling. 

And then, if I do finally drift off, that's when the dreams come. Or, if it ain't the dreams, it's just... little noises. A branch scraping the window, or a car driving by too fast down on Elm Street. Just the smallest little sound, and my eyes snap open, wide awake, heart beating a little too fast. And once I'm up, whether it's from one of those awful dreams or just a noise in the house... I can't seem to settle back down. I just can't do it. My mind starts racing, and the bed feels too hot, or too cold, or the sheets feel tangled up. So I end up getting out of bed and going out to the living room to sit in my recliner. 

So, how often? It feels like nearly every night, doc. It really does. Some nights are a little better than others, maybe I get a couple hours straight before I wake up, but it's a rare, rare thing for me to just sleep through the night anymore. It's just... I lie there, I wake up, and I can't get back to sleep. That's the long and short of it. I just want to be able to close my eyes and rest like a normal person again, you know?

But... well, mostly I'm here because I just don't sleep right anymore. It's been driving me absolutely crazy. \n\nI go to bed, I got my room all set up, nice and quiet, but I just lie there. I lie there for a really long time, tossing and turning, just staring up at the ceiling and watching the shadows from the streetlamp outside. And then, if I do finally manage to drift off, it ain't a peaceful rest. I end up waking up from these bad dreams, or sometimes it's just... little noises. Like a floorboard creaking, or the wind hitting the window, or a car driving by a block over. The tiniest little sound and my eyes just pop wide open. \n\nAnd the worst part is, once I'm awake like that, I can't seem to settle back down. I try getting up, sitting in my recliner in the living room, maybe having a glass of water, but I just can't get back to sleep no matter what I do. Night after night, it's just... it wears a fella out, not getting any proper rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried around bedtime, or does your mind often race with thoughts when you're trying to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that. I ain't really a worrier, never have been. I mean, I ain't sitting up in bed fretting about the electric bill or if the roof is gonna leak or nothing like that. My mind ain't racing at all. \n\nUsually, I just watch a little television in the evening—maybe the local news or one of them old Westerns on the classic channel—and I feel right calm, you know? I turn off the set, go in the bathroom to wash my face, brush my teeth... still got most of my own teeth, which ain't too bad for a fella my age... and I get into bed. I get under the covers and my head is just plain quiet. I ain't anxious. I might lay there and think about, oh, I don't know, what I'm gonna make for breakfast the next morning, or if I ought to sweep the front porch, just regular everyday stuff. \n\nIt ain't racing. It's just... wide awake, if you catch my drift. It's like my brain just don't know it's supposed to shut off. So I just lay there, staring at that ceiling, waiting for a sleep that just won't come. And then, like I was saying before, that's when I start hearing every little creak and groan of the house...\n\nDoctor: Do you usually wake up earlier than you'd like to, and if so, do you find it hard to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, early... see, I don't know about early, exactly. It ain't like I'm waking up at four in the morning when the roosters crow, not that we got roosters around my neighborhood anymore. Old man Miller down the street used to have a few chickens in his backyard, oh, maybe twenty or twenty-five years ago, but the city made him get rid of 'em because of the noise. Caused a whole big fuss, people complaining and all that. \n\nAnyway, no, I wouldn't say I wake up earlier than I want to in the morning. My regular time to get out of bed has always been about six-thirty, maybe seven. Even back when I was working, doing manual labor, I'd be up by six, so my body is just plain used to that. If I actually make it to the morning, I just get up at my regular time, put my slippers on, and head to the kitchen to get the coffee going. \n\nThe trouble, see, like I was trying to tell you before, is the middle of the night. It ain't the morning that's the problem. It's the two in the morning, three in the morning wake-ups. That's when I pop awake from a bad dream or a floorboard groaning, and then I'm just stuck. I'll go sit in my recliner in the living room, maybe look out the front window at that streetlamp I mentioned, just watching the moths flying around the light. I just sit there for hours, rubbing my knees, wishing I was asleep. But as for the actual morning time? No, I don't wake up too early then. If I'm lucky enough to be asleep when the sun starts coming up, I'll just sleep right till seven like I always have.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling tired or sleepy during the day because of these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: Well, you'd think I would be, wouldn't you? I just go about my day. I'll go sit in my recliner in the living room, maybe look out the front window at that streetlamp I mentioned, just watching the moths flying around the light. I just sit there for hours, rubbing my knees, wishing I was asleep. But as for the actual morning time? No, I don't wake up too early then. If I'm lucky enough to be asleep when the sun starts coming up, I'll just sleep right till seven like I always have.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling tired or sleepy during the day because of these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: Well, you'd think I would be, wouldn't you? What with all that sitting up in the dark and pacing around the living room. But honestly... no, I ain't really sleepy during the daytime. It's the strangest thing. \n\nI mean, you take my brother-in-law, Frank, for example. He's a year younger than me, seventy-three, and he sleeps like a log all night, but come two in the afternoon, he's dozing off in his armchair. Can't even make it through a whole episode of the game shows without his chin hitting his chest and him snoring away. But me? I've never been much of a napper. Never really had the time for it back when I was working, so I guess I just never got the habit. \n\nEven with the bad sleep at night now, once the sun is up and I get that first cup of coffee in me, I'm just... I'm awake. I might be a little stiff, you know, my knees and my back ain't exactly what they used to be from all those years hauling things around the work site, but I ain't yawning or feeling like my eyes are heavy. I just go about my day. I'll read the morning paper, maybe go out and tinker a bit in the garage—I got this old push mower I've been meaning to fix up, just needs a little cleaning out, I figure—and I just keep myself moving. So, no, doc, I don't really get tired or sleepy when the sun's out. It's just when the sun goes down that the trouble starts.\n\nDoctor: When you're lying in bed or sitting quietly at night, do you ever feel an uncomfortable sensation in your legs or a strong urge to move them?\n\nPatient: Well, no... no, I can't say that I do. My legs... well, like I said, my knees ache a fair bit, especially when it's damp out or if I've been standing on the concrete floor in the garage too long. That concrete is mighty hard on the joints, you know. I used to wear these thick work boots with the extra padding, but nowadays I just wear my old sneakers, and they don't give much support. \n\nBut as for an uncomfortable feeling... like an urge to move 'em? No, nothing like that. When I'm sitting in my recliner in the middle of the night, my legs are perfectly still. I mean, I might rub my knees, like I told you, just to work the stiffness out, or maybe I'll cross one ankle over the other, but they ain't twitching or restless or nothing. They just sit there like the rest of me, wide awake and waiting for the morning. \n\nI did know a fella once, guy named Earl I worked with down at the lumber yard... oh, must be thirty years ago now. He used to say his legs felt like they had bugs crawling inside 'em at night. Said he had to get up and walk around the block at two in the morning just to get 'em to settle down. His wife thought he was crazy, going out for a stroll in his pajamas. But me? No, my legs are fine, just a bit stiff from the years. It's just my brain that won't let me sleep, not my legs.\n\nDoctor: Do you snore loudly or have anyone ever told you that you stop breathing or gasp for air during the night?\n\nPatient: Snoring? Well, now... see, since my Mary passed—Lord, it's been six years this November—I've been sleeping alone, mostly. So there ain't nobody right there next to me to complain about it if I do. But even back when she was alive, she never said much about it. Now, my brother-in-law Frank, the one I was telling you about who falls asleep watching the game shows? Oh, he snores like a freight train. You can hear him clear on the other side of the house. His wife, my sister Shirley, she actually makes him sleep in the guest room sometimes when his allergies act up because it gets so loud it rattles the picture frames on the nightstand. \n\nBut me? No, Mary never said I snored loud or nothing. She was a light sleeper, too. If the wind blew too hard against the siding, she'd wake up. But she never poked me in the ribs and told me to roll over or said I was making a racket. And as for stopping breathing or gasping... well, I don't think so. I mean, I'm awake half the night anyway, just sitting there in the dark watching the moths, so I think I'd notice if I suddenly couldn't catch my breath, right? I ain't ever woken up choking or gasping for air. The only thing that wakes me up in a panic is... well, the bad dreams, like I said. But it ain't my breathing that's the problem. It's just my mind running away with me. So, no, doc, I don't think I got any of that going on.\n\nDoctor: How often do these bad dreams happen—nearly every night, a few times a week, or less often?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... that's the whole reason I'm sitting here in this chair today, to be honest with you. Mostly I'm here because I just don't sleep right anymore. It ain't just once in a blue moon, neither. It's... well, it feels like it's all the time. I'll get myself ready for bed, you know, wash my face, brush my teeth, check the locks on the doors—always gotta check the locks, maybe twice, just to be sure—and then I'll get under the covers. And I just lie there. For a long, long time. I'll look at the clock, and it'll be eleven, then twelve, then one. Just staring at the ceiling. \n\nAnd then, if I do finally drift off, that's when the dreams come. Or, if it ain't the dreams, it's just... little noises. A branch scraping the window, or a car driving by too fast down on Elm Street. Just the smallest little sound, and my eyes snap open, wide awake, heart beating a little too fast. And once I'm up, whether it's from one of those awful dreams or just a noise in the house... I can't seem to settle back down. I just can't do it. My mind starts racing, and the bed feels too hot, or too cold, or the sheets feel tangled up. So I end up getting out of bed and going out to the living room to sit in my recliner. \n\nSo, how often? It feels like nearly every night, doc. It really does. Some nights are a little better than others, maybe I get a couple hours straight before I wake up, but it's a rare, rare thing for me to just sleep through the night anymore. It's just... I lie there, I wake up, and I can't get back to sleep. That's the long and short of it. I just want to be able to close my eyes and rest like a normal person again, you know?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doc, the dreams... they're always about the same thing. Always the same awful thing. It’s... well, it’s about the accident. You see, I worked construction most of my life, doing all sorts of jobs, mostly framing and roofing. Hard work, honest work. But this one day, we were working on this big commercial building over on the east side of town—must have been, oh, maybe fifteen years ago now. We were putting up some heavy steel beams, and... well, something gave way. I still don't know exactly what happened, if a cable snapped or what, but the whole thing came down. 

I was underneath, and a piece of the scaffolding collapsed right on top of me. Pinned me down good. I was stuck there for I don't know how long, felt like hours, just smelling the dust and the rust, hearing the metal groaning, wondering if the rest of it was going to come down and finish the job. That’s what the dreams are about. I’m back under there, and it’s dark, and I can’t breathe, and I can’t move my legs. And then I wake up, and my heart is just hammering against my ribs, like a bird trapped in a cage, you know? I’m sweating through my pajamas, and I just feel this... this awful panic, like the ceiling of my own bedroom is about to cave in on me. 

And the thing is, doc, it ain’t just the dreams. It’s not just when I’m sleeping. It happens when I’m wide awake, too. Like, I’ll be walking down the street, maybe going to the hardware store for some nails or something, and I’ll hear a sound. Maybe it’s two pieces of metal scraping together, like a truck hitting a bump, or a gate swinging shut. Or maybe I’ll just see a ladder leaning up against somebody's house, or a pile of pipes sitting in a yard. And just like that, bam. It’s like a switch flips in my brain. Suddenly, I ain't on the sidewalk anymore. The whole thing jumps back in my head, clear as day, like I’m right back under that scaffolding again, waiting for the rescue crew, waiting for help that feels like it’s never going to come. I can almost smell the dust again. It just takes over, you know? And I have to just stand there for a minute and remind myself where I am, that I’m safe, that it’s over. But it doesn't feel over, doc. It really doesn't.
=====================================

And then, if I do finally manage to drift off, it ain't a peaceful rest. I end up waking up from these bad dreams, or sometimes it's just... little noises. Like a floorboard creaking, or the wind hitting the window, or a car driving by a block over. The tiniest little sound and my eyes just pop wide open. \n\nAnd the worst part is, once I'm awake like that, I can't seem to settle back down. I try getting up, sitting in my recliner in the living room, maybe having a glass of water, but I just can't get back to sleep no matter what I do. Night after night, it's just... it wears a fella out, not getting any proper rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried around bedtime, or does your mind often race with thoughts when you're trying to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that. I ain't really a worrier, never have been. I mean, I ain't sitting up in bed fretting about the electric bill or if the roof is gonna leak or nothing like that. My mind ain't racing at all. \n\nUsually, I just watch a little television in the evening—maybe the local news or one of them old Westerns on the classic channel—and I feel right calm, you know? I turn off the set, go in the bathroom to wash my face, brush my teeth... still got most of my own teeth, which ain't too bad for a fella my age... and I get into bed. I get under the covers and my head is just plain quiet. I ain't anxious. I might lay there and think about, oh, I don't know, what I'm gonna make for breakfast the next morning, or if I ought to sweep the front porch, just regular everyday stuff. \n\nIt ain't racing. It's just... wide awake, if you catch my drift. It's like my brain just don't know it's supposed to shut off. So I just lay there, staring at that ceiling, waiting for a sleep that just won't come. And then, like I was saying before, that's when I start hearing every little creak and groan of the house...\n\nDoctor: Do you usually wake up earlier than you'd like to, and if so, do you find it hard to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, early... see, I don't know about early, exactly. It ain't like I'm waking up at four in the morning when the roosters crow, not that we got roosters around my neighborhood anymore. Old man Miller down the street used to have a few chickens in his backyard, oh, maybe twenty or twenty-five years ago, but the city made him get rid of 'em because of the noise. Caused a whole big fuss, people complaining and all that. \n\nAnyway, no, I wouldn't say I wake up earlier than I want to in the morning. My regular time to get out of bed has always been about six-thirty, maybe seven. Even back when I was working, doing manual labor, I'd be up by six, so my body is just plain used to that. If I actually make it to the morning, I just get up at my regular time, put my slippers on, and head to the kitchen to get the coffee going. \n\nThe trouble, see, like I was trying to tell you before, is the middle of the night. It ain't the morning that's the problem. It's the two in the morning, three in the morning wake-ups. That's when I pop awake from a bad dream or a floorboard groaning, and then I'm just stuck. I'll go sit in my recliner in the living room, maybe look out the front window at that streetlamp I mentioned, just watching the moths flying around the light. I just sit there for hours, rubbing my knees, wishing I was asleep. But as for the actual morning time? No, I don't wake up too early then. If I'm lucky enough to be asleep when the sun starts coming up, I'll just sleep right till seven like I always have.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling tired or sleepy during the day because of these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: Well, you'd think I would be, wouldn't you? What with all that sitting up in the dark and pacing around the living room. But honestly... no, I ain't really sleepy during the daytime. It's the strangest thing. \n\nI mean, you take my brother-in-law, Frank, for example. He's a year younger than me, seventy-three, and he sleeps like a log all night, but come two in the afternoon, he's dozing off in his armchair. Can't even make it through a whole episode of the game shows without his chin hitting his chest and him snoring away. But me? I've never been much of a napper. Never really had the time for it back when I was working, so I guess I just never got the habit. \n\nEven with the bad sleep at night now, once the sun is up and I get that first cup of coffee in me, I'm just... I'm awake. I might be a little stiff, you know, my knees and my back ain't exactly what they used to be from all those years hauling things around the work site, but I ain't yawning or feeling like my eyes are heavy. I just go about my day. I'll read the morning paper, maybe go out and tinker a bit in the garage—I got this old push mower I've been meaning to fix up, just needs a little cleaning out, I figure—and I just keep myself moving. So, no, doc, I don't really get tired or sleepy when the sun's out. It's just when the sun goes down that the trouble starts.\n\nDoctor: When you're lying in bed or sitting quietly at night, do you ever feel an uncomfortable sensation in your legs or a strong urge to move them?\n\nPatient: Well, no... no, I can't say that I do. My legs... well, like I said, my knees ache a fair bit, especially when it's damp out or if I've been standing on the concrete floor in the garage too long. That concrete is mighty hard on the joints, you know. I used to wear these thick work boots with the extra padding, but nowadays I just wear my old sneakers, and they don't give much support. \n\nBut as for an uncomfortable feeling... like an urge to move 'em? No, nothing like that. When I'm sitting in my recliner in the middle of the night, my legs are perfectly still. I mean, I might rub my knees, like I told you, just to work the stiffness out, or maybe I'll cross one ankle over the other, but they ain't twitching or restless or nothing. They just sit there like the rest of me, wide awake and waiting for the morning. \n\nI did know a fella once, guy named Earl I worked with down at the lumber yard... oh, must be thirty years ago now. He used to say his legs felt like they had bugs crawling inside 'em at night. Said he had to get up and walk around the block at two in the morning just to get 'em to settle down. His wife thought he was crazy, going out for a stroll in his pajamas. But me? No, my legs are fine, just a bit stiff from the years. It's just my brain that won't let me sleep, not my legs.\n\nDoctor: Do you snore loudly or have anyone ever told you that you stop breathing or gasp for air during the night?\n\nPatient: Snoring? Well, now... see, since my Mary passed—Lord, it's been six years this November—I've been sleeping alone, mostly. So there ain't nobody right there next to me to complain about it if I do. But even back when she was alive, she never said much about it. Now, my brother-in-law Frank, the one I was telling you about who falls asleep watching the game shows? Oh, he snores like a freight train. You can hear him clear on the other side of the house. His wife, my sister Shirley, she actually makes him sleep in the guest room sometimes when his allergies act up because it gets so loud it rattles the picture frames on the nightstand. \n\nBut me? No, Mary never said I snored loud or nothing. She was a light sleeper, too. If the wind blew too hard against the siding, she'd wake up. But she never poked me in the ribs and told me to roll over or said I was making a racket. And as for stopping breathing or gasping... well, I don't think so. I mean, I'm awake half the night anyway, just sitting there in the dark watching the moths, so I think I'd notice if I suddenly couldn't catch my breath, right? I ain't ever woken up choking or gasping for air. The only thing that wakes me up in a panic is... well, the bad dreams, like I said. But it ain't my breathing that's the problem. It's just my mind running away with me. So, no, doc, I don't think I got any of that going on.\n\nDoctor: How often do these bad dreams happen—nearly every night, a few times a week, or less often?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... that's the whole reason I'm sitting here in this chair today, to be honest with you. Mostly I'm here because I just don't sleep right anymore. It ain't just once in a blue moon, neither. It's... well, it feels like it's all the time. I'll get myself ready for bed, you know, wash my face, brush my teeth, check the locks on the doors—always gotta check the locks, maybe twice, just to be sure—and then I'll get under the covers. And I just lie there. For a long, long time. I'll look at the clock, and it'll be eleven, then twelve, then one. Just staring at the ceiling. \n\nAnd then, if I do finally drift off, that's when the dreams come. Or, if it ain't the dreams, it's just... little noises. A branch scraping the window, or a car driving by too fast down on Elm Street. Just the smallest little sound, and my eyes snap open, wide awake, heart beating a little too fast. And once I'm up, whether it's from one of those awful dreams or just a noise in the house... I can't seem to settle back down. I just can't do it. My mind starts racing, and the bed feels too hot, or too cold, or the sheets feel tangled up. So I end up getting out of bed and going out to the living room to sit in my recliner. \n\nSo, how often? It feels like nearly every night, doc. It really does. Some nights are a little better than others, maybe I get a couple hours straight before I wake up, but it's a rare, rare thing for me to just sleep through the night anymore. It's just... I lie there, I wake up, and I can't get back to sleep. That's the long and short of it. I just want to be able to close my eyes and rest like a normal person again, you know?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me a little about these bad dreams you mentioned—what they’re about and how they make you feel when you wake up?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, the dreams... they're always about the same thing. Always the same awful thing. It’s... well, it’s about the accident. You see, I worked construction most of my life, doing all sorts of jobs, mostly framing and roofing. Hard work, honest work. But this one day, we were working on this big commercial building over on the east side of town—must have been, oh, maybe fifteen years ago now. We were putting up some heavy steel beams, and... well, something gave way. I still don't know exactly what happened, if a cable snapped or what, but the whole thing came down. \n\nI was underneath, and a piece of the scaffolding collapsed right on top of me. Pinned me down good. I was stuck there for I don't know how long, felt like hours, just smelling the dust and the rust, hearing the metal groaning, wondering if the rest of it was going to come down and finish the job. That’s what the dreams are about. I’m back under there, and it’s dark, and I can’t breathe, and I can’t move my legs. And then I wake up, and my heart is just hammering against my ribs, like a bird trapped in a cage, you know? I’m sweating through my pajamas, and I just feel this... this awful panic, like the ceiling of my own bedroom is about to cave in on me. \n\nAnd the thing is, doc, it ain’t just the dreams. It’s not just when I’m sleeping. It happens when I’m wide awake, too. Like, I’ll be walking down the street, maybe going to the hardware store for some nails or something, and I’ll hear a sound. Maybe it’s two pieces of metal scraping together, like a truck hitting a bump, or a gate swinging shut. Or maybe I’ll just see a ladder leaning up against somebody's house, or a pile of pipes sitting in a yard. And just like that, bam. It’s like a switch flips in my brain. Suddenly, I ain't on the sidewalk anymore. The whole thing jumps back in my head, clear as day, like I’m right back under that scaffolding again, waiting for the rescue crew, waiting for help that feels like it’s never going to come. I can almost smell the dust again. It just takes over, you know? And I have to just stand there for a minute and remind myself where I am, that I’m safe, that it’s over. But it doesn't feel over, doc. It really doesn't.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  "candidates": ["F43.10", "G47.00"],
  "note": "The patient provided detailed descriptions of recurrent nightmares related to a past traumatic event (construction accident), as well as intrusive thoughts and flashbacks triggered by external stimuli. These symptoms strongly suggest Posttraumatic Stress Disorder (PTSD), which is now added as a candidate (F43.10). Insomnia disorder (G47.00) remains a candidate due to the persistent difficulty falling and staying asleep. The patient does not endorse symptoms of anxiety at bedtime, ruling out anxiety-related sleep disturbance as a primary cause. No evidence of substance use, mood episodes, or other primary sleep disorders such as sleep apnea or restless legs syndrome were endorsed.",
  "is_final": false
}

You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S027\n  name: Sleep_Disturbance\n  description: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.\n  manifestation: Mostly I'm here because I don't sleep right anymore; I lie there a long time, then wake up from bad dreams or little noises and can't seem to settle back down.\n- key: S069\n  name: Intrusive_Symptoms\n  description: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.\n  manifestation: Sometimes when I hear metal scrape or see a ladder leaning in somebody's yard, the whole thing jumps back in my head like I'm under there again waiting for help.\n- key: S070\n  name: Trauma_Avoidance\n  description: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.\n  manifestation: I keep finding reasons not to go to the senior center or work around the garage, especially near the tools and the spot where I used to keep that ladder.\n- key: S071\n  name: Trauma_Related_Amnesia\n  description: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).\n  manifestation: I remember helping with the decorations and I remember the folks talking to me after, but the part where I hit the ground is just missing, like somebody cut it out.\n- key: S072\n  name: Negative_Beliefs\n  description: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.\n  manifestation: I know it may sound foolish, but now I keep others.\n  manifestation: My wife will be sitting right there and the bridge fellows will be joking around, but I feel like I'm behind a window and not really with them.\n- key: S076\n  name: Well, now... see, since my Mary passed—Lord, it's been six years this November—I've been sleeping alone, mostly. So there ain't nobody right there next to me to complain about it if I do. But even back when she was alive, she never said much about it. Now, my brother-in-law Frank, the one I was telling you about who falls asleep watching the game shows? Oh, he snores like a freight train. You can hear him clear on the other side of the house. His wife, my sister Shirley, she actually makes him sleep in the guest room sometimes when his allergies act up because it gets so loud it rattles the picture frames on the nightstand. \n\nBut me? No, Mary never said I snored loud or nothing. She was a light sleeper, too. If the wind blew too hard against the siding, she'd wake up. But she never poked me in the ribs and told me to roll over or said I was making a racket. And as for stopping breathing or gasping... well, I don't think so. I mean, I'm awake half the night anyway, just sitting there in the dark watching the moths, so I think I'd notice if I suddenly couldn't catch my breath, right? I ain't ever woken up choking or gasping for air. The only thing that wakes me up in a panic is... well, the bad dreams, like I said. But it ain't my breathing that's the problem. It's just my mind running away with me. So, no, doc, I don't think I got any of that going on.\n\nDoctor: How often do these bad dreams happen—nearly every night, a few times a week, or less often?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... that's the whole reason I'm sitting here in this chair today, to be honest with you. Always the same awful thing. It’s... well, it’s about the accident. You see, I worked construction most of my life, doing all sorts of jobs, mostly framing and roofing. Hard work, honest work. But this one day, we were working on this big commercial building over on the east side of town—must have been, oh, maybe fifteen years ago now. We were putting up some heavy steel beams, and... well, something gave way. I still don't know exactly what happened, if a cable snapped or what, but the whole thing came down. \n\nI was underneath, and a piece of the scaffolding collapsed right on top of me. Pinned me down good. I was stuck there for I don't know how long, felt like hours, just smelling the dust and the rust, hearing the metal groaning, wondering if the rest of it was going to come down and finish the job. That’s what the dreams are about. I’m back under there, and it’s dark, and I can’t breathe, and I can’t move my legs. And then I wake up, and my heart is just hammering against my ribs, like a bird trapped in a cage, you know? I’m sweating through my pajamas, and I just feel this... this awful panic, like the ceiling of my own bedroom is about to cave in on me. \n\nAnd the thing is, doc, it ain’t just the dreams. It’s not just when I’m sleeping. It happens when I’m wide awake, too. Like, I’ll be walking down the street, maybe going to the hardware store for some nails or something, and I’ll hear a sound. Suddenly, I ain't on the sidewalk anymore. The whole thing jumps back in my head, clear as day, like I’m right back under that scaffolding again, waiting for the rescue crew, waiting for help that feels like it’s never going to come. I can almost smell the dust again. It just takes over, you know? And I have to just stand there for a minute and remind myself where I am, that I’m safe, that it’s over. But it doesn't feel over, doc. It really doesn't.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself feeling on edge, easily startled, or having trouble concentrating during the day because of these memories from the accident?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Well, doc, on edge ain't even the half of it. It's like... like my nerves are just sitting right there on the outside of my skin, if you know what I mean. I’m just wound up tight as a cheap watch all day long. And startled? Oh, Lord, yes. It don't take much at all anymore. 

Just the other day, I was down at the grocery store—just that little one down the street, trying to find some of that low-sodium chicken noodle soup because they tell me I gotta watch my salt now at my age—and I was just standing there in the aisle, squinting at the label. And this young fella, I guess he worked there, he just came up behind me to put some boxes on the shelf. He didn't mean no harm, didn't make much noise, but he just stepped up right behind me. 

Well, I tell you, I jumped so hard I nearly dropped the can right there on the linoleum. It's like that with everything now. If a door bangs shut somewhere in the house, or if the wind catches the screen, or if someone just comes up behind me like that grocery boy... I jump hard. I just about jump clear out of my shoes. And the worst part of it is, even after I turn around and see it's just a kid with a box of soup, or just the wind blowing a door, my chest just keeps on thumping. It just thumps and thumps for a good long while. I stand there and tell myself, "You're fine, old man, it's just a noise, it's nothing," but my body just don't listen. It just keeps racing even after I know for a fact it's nothing to worry about. 

Takes me a good ten, fifteen minutes just to get my breath back regular and stop my hands from shaking. So, you can imagine, trying to concentrate on much of anything, whether it's reading the paper or fixing a leaky faucet, when you're jumping at shadows like that... well, it ain't easy. You just sit there waiting for the next thing to bang or crash.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello there, doc. I, uh... I guess I should just start by saying I wasn't entirely sure about coming in today. You get to be my age, seventy-four years old, and you just sort of figure things are gonna break down or not work the way they used to, you know? But... well, mostly I'm here because I just don't sleep right anymore. It's been driving me absolutely crazy. \n\nI go to bed, I got my room all set up, nice and quiet, but I just lie there. I lie there for a really long time, tossing and turning, just staring up at the ceiling and watching the shadows from the streetlamp outside. And then, if I do finally manage to drift off, it ain't a peaceful rest. I end up waking up from these bad dreams, or sometimes it's just... little noises. Like a floorboard creaking, or the wind hitting the window, or a car driving by a block over. The tiniest little sound and my eyes just pop wide open. \n\nAnd the worst part is, once I'm awake like that, I can't seem to settle back down. I try getting up, sitting in my recliner in the living room, maybe having a glass of water, but I just can't get back to sleep no matter what I do. Night after night, it's just... it wears a fella out, not getting any proper rest.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried around bedtime, or does your mind often race with thoughts when you're trying to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that. I ain't really a worrier, never have been. I mean, I ain't sitting up in bed fretting about the electric bill or if the roof is gonna leak or nothing like that. My mind ain't racing at all. \n\nUsually, I just watch a little television in the evening—maybe the local news or one of them old Westerns on the classic channel—and I feel right calm, you know? I turn off the set, go in the bathroom to wash my face, brush my teeth... still got most of my own teeth, which ain't too bad for a fella my age... and I get into bed. I get under the covers and my head is just plain quiet. I ain't anxious. I might lay there and think about, oh, I don't know, what I'm gonna make for breakfast the next morning, or if I ought to sweep the front porch, just regular everyday stuff. \n\nIt ain't racing. It's just... wide awake, if you catch my drift. It's like my brain just don't know it's supposed to shut off. So I just lay there, staring at that ceiling, waiting for a sleep that just won't come. It ain't like I'm waking up at four in the morning when the roosters crow, not that we got roosters around my neighborhood anymore. Old man Miller down the street used to have a few chickens in his backyard, oh, maybe twenty or twenty-five years ago, but the city made him get rid of 'em because of the noise. Caused a whole big fuss, people complaining and all that. \n\nAnyway, no, I wouldn't say I wake up earlier than I want to in the morning. My regular time to get out of bed has always been about six-thirty, maybe seven. Even back when I was working, doing manual labor, I'd be up by six, so my body is just plain used to that. If I actually make it to the morning, I just get up at my regular time, put my slippers on, and head to the kitchen to get the coffee going. \n\nThe trouble, see, like I was trying to tell you before, is the middle of the night. It ain't the morning that's the problem. It's the two in the morning, three in the morning wake-ups. That's when I pop awake from a bad dream or a floorboard groaning, and then I'm just stuck. I'll go sit in my recliner in the living room, maybe look out the front window at that streetlamp I mentioned, just watching the moths flying around the light. I just sit there for hours, rubbing my knees, wishing I was asleep. But as for the actual morning time? No, I don't wake up too early then. If I'm lucky enough to be asleep when the sun starts coming up, I'll just sleep right till seven like I always have.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling tired or sleepy during the day because of these sleep problems?\n\nPatient: Well, you'd think I would be, wouldn't you? What with all that sitting up in the dark and pacing around the living room. But honestly... no, I ain't really sleepy during the daytime. It's the strangest thing. \n\nI mean, you take my brother-in-law, Frank, for example. He's a year younger than me, seventy-three, and he sleeps like a log all night, but come two in the afternoon, he's dozing off in his armchair. Can't even make it through a whole episode of the game shows without his chin hitting his chest and him snoring away. But me? I've never been much of a napper. Never really had the time for it back when I was working, so I guess I just never got the habit. \n\nEven with the bad sleep at night now, once the sun is up and I get that first cup of coffee in me, I'm just... I'm awake. I might be a little stiff, you know, my knees and my back ain't exactly what they used to be from all those years hauling things around the work site, but I ain't yawning or feeling like my eyes are heavy. I just go about my day. I'll read the morning paper, maybe go out and tinker a bit in the garage—I got this old push mower I've been meaning to fix up, just needs a little cleaning out, I figure—and I just keep myself moving. So, no, doc, I don't really get tired or sleepy when the sun's out. It's just when the sun goes down that the trouble starts.\n\nDoctor: When you're lying in bed or sitting quietly at night, do you ever feel an uncomfortable sensation in your legs or a strong urge to move them?\n\nPatient: Well, no... no, I can't say that I do. My legs... well, like I said, my knees ache a fair bit, especially when it's damp out or if I've been standing on the concrete floor in the garage too long. That concrete is mighty hard on the joints, you know. I used to wear these thick work boots with the extra padding, but nowadays I just wear my old sneakers, and they don't give much support. \n\nBut as for an uncomfortable feeling... like an urge to move 'em? No, nothing like that. When I'm sitting in my recliner in the middle of the night, my legs are perfectly still. I mean, I might rub my knees, like I told you, just to work the stiffness out, or maybe I'll cross one ankle over the other, but they ain't twitching or restless or nothing. They just sit there like the rest of me, wide awake and waiting for the morning. \n\nI did know a fella once, guy named Earl I worked with down at the lumber yard... oh, must be thirty years ago now. He used to say his legs felt like they had bugs crawling inside 'em at night. Said he had to get up and walk around the block at two in the morning just to get 'em to settle down. His wife thought he was crazy, going out for a stroll in his pajamas. But me? No, my legs are fine, just a bit stiff from the years. It's just my brain that won't let me sleep, not my legs.\n\nDoctor: Do you snore loudly or have anyone ever told you that you stop breathing or gasp for air during the night?\n\nPatient: Snoring? Well, now... see, since my Mary passed—Lord, it's been six years this November—I've been sleeping alone, mostly. So there ain't nobody right there next to me to complain about it if I do. But even back when she was alive, she never said much about it. Now, my brother-in-law Frank, the one I was telling you about who falls asleep watching the game shows? Oh, he snores like a freight train. You can hear him clear on the other side of the house. No, Mary never said I snored loud or nothing. She was a light sleeper, too. If the wind blew too hard against the siding, she'd wake up. I mean, I'm awake half the night anyway, just sitting there in the dark watching the moths, so I think I'd notice if I suddenly couldn't catch my breath, right? I ain't ever woken up choking or gasping for air. The only thing that wakes me up in a panic is... well, the bad dreams, like I said. But it ain't my breathing that's the problem. It's just my mind running away with me. So, no, doc, I don't think I got any of that going on.\n\nDoctor: How often do these bad dreams happen—nearly every night, a few times a week, or less often?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... that's the whole reason I'm sitting here in this chair today, to be honest with you. Mostly I'm here because I just don't sleep right anymore. It ain't just once in a blue moon, neither. It's... well, it feels like it's all the time. I'll get myself ready for bed, you know, wash my face, brush my teeth, check the locks on the doors—always gotta check the locks, maybe twice, just to be sure—and then I'll get under the covers. And I just lie there. For a long, long time. I'll look at the clock, and it'll be eleven, then twelve, then one. Just staring at the ceiling. \n\nAnd then, if I do finally drift off, that's when the dreams come. Or, if it ain't the dreams, it's just... little noises. A branch scraping the window, or a car driving by too fast down on Elm Street. Just the smallest little sound, and my eyes snap open, wide awake, heart beating a little too fast. And once I'm up, whether it's from one of those awful dreams or just a noise in the house... I can't seem to settle back down. I just can't do it. My mind starts racing, and the bed feels too hot, or too cold, or the sheets feel tangled up. So I end up getting out of bed and going out to the living room to sit in my recliner. \n\nSo, how often? It feels like nearly every night, doc. It really does. Some nights are a little better than others, maybe I get a couple hours straight before I wake up, but it's a rare, rare thing for me to just sleep through the night anymore. It's just... I lie there, I wake up, and I can't get back to sleep. That's the long and short of it. I just want to be able to close my eyes and rest like a normal person again, you know?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me a little about these bad dreams you mentioned—what they’re about and how they make you feel when you wake up?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, the dreams... they're always about the same thing. Always the same awful thing. It’s... well, it’s about the accident. You see, I worked construction most of my life, doing all sorts of jobs, mostly framing and roofing. Hard work, honest work. But this one day, we were working on this big commercial building over on the east side of town—must have been, oh, maybe fifteen years ago now. We were putting up some heavy steel beams, and... well, something gave way. I still don't know exactly what happened, if a cable snapped or what, but the whole thing came down. \n\nI was underneath, and a piece of the scaffolding collapsed right on top of me. Pinned me down good. I was stuck there for I don't know how long, felt like hours, just smelling the dust and the rust, hearing the metal groaning, wondering if the rest of it was going to come down and finish the job. That’s what the dreams are about. I’m back under there, and it’s dark, and I can’t breathe, and I can’t move my legs. And then I wake up, and my heart is just hammering against my ribs, like a bird trapped in a cage, you know? I’m sweating through my pajamas, and I just feel this... this awful panic, like the ceiling of my own bedroom is about to cave in on me. \n\nAnd the thing is, doc, it ain’t just the dreams. It’s not just when I’m sleeping. It happens when I’m wide awake, too. Like, I’ll be walking down the street, maybe going to the hardware store for some nails or something, and I’ll hear a sound. Maybe it’s two pieces of metal scraping together, like a truck hitting a bump, or a gate swinging shut. Or maybe I’ll just see a ladder leaning up against somebody's house, or a pile of pipes sitting in a yard. And just like that, bam. It’s like a switch flips in my brain. Suddenly, I ain't on the sidewalk anymore. The whole thing jumps back in my head, clear as day, like I’m right back under that scaffolding again, waiting for the rescue crew, waiting for help that feels like it’s never going to come. I can almost smell the dust again. It just takes over, you know? And I have to just stand there for a minute and remind myself where I am, that I’m safe, that it’s over. But it doesn't feel over, doc. It really doesn't.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling on edge, easily startled, or having trouble concentrating during the day because of these memories from the accident?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, on edge ain't even the half of it. It's like... like my nerves are just sitting right there on the outside of my skin, if you know what I mean. I’m just wound up tight as a cheap watch all day long. And startled? Oh, Lord, yes. It don't take much at all anymore. \n\nJust the other day, I was down at the grocery store—just that little one down the street, trying to find some of that low-sodium chicken noodle soup because they tell me I gotta watch my salt now at my age—and I was just standing there in the aisle, squinting at the label. And this young fella, I guess he worked there, he just came up behind me to put some boxes on the shelf. He didn't mean no harm, didn't make much noise, but he just stepped up right behind me. \n\nWell, I tell you, I jumped so hard I nearly dropped the can right there on the linoleum. It's like that with everything now. If a door bangs shut somewhere in the house, or if the wind catches the screen, or if someone just comes up behind me like that grocery boy... I jump hard. I just about jump clear out of my shoes. And the worst part of it is, even after I turn around and see it's just a kid with a box of soup, or just the wind blowing a door, my chest just keeps on thumping. It just thumps and thumps for a good long while. I stand there and tell myself, \"You're fine, old man, it's just a noise, it's nothing,\" but my body just don't listen. It just keeps racing even after I know for a fact it's nothing to worry about. \n\nTakes me a good ten, fifteen minutes just to get my breath back regular and stop my hands from shaking. So, you can imagine, trying to concentrate on much of anything, whether it's reading the paper or fixing a leaky faucet, when you're jumping at shadows like that... well, it ain't easy. You just sit there waiting for the next thing to bang or crash.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
