Yeah, I got a lot of fear in me now. But the strange thing is, it ain't just fear about the accident itself anymore. It's... well, it's sort of bled into everything else in my life. \n\nLet me try and explain it to you. You see, when you work with your hands for forty-some years, you learn to trust the things around you. You trust a good piece of oak, you trust the nails you drive, you trust the ground you're standing on. If you build it right, it holds. That's just how the world is supposed to work. But since that day... well, it's like the rules all changed on me. \n\nI know it may sound foolish, an old man talking like this, but now I keep thinking everyday things aren't safe. I really do. It’s like... if I trust something simple, it'll turn on me. Take the front porch steps at my house, for instance. I poured that concrete myself back in '78. Mixed it right there in the yard, smoothed it out perfect. Solid as a rock. But now, I go to step out in the morning to grab the newspaper, and I look down at that concrete, and my stomach just drops. I start thinking, what if it just crumbles? What if the dirt underneath it washed away in the rain and the whole thing is just waiting to collapse under my boots? \n\nOr... or the ceiling fan in the living room. My wife bought that at Sears twenty years ago. It's been spinning up there all this time, keeping the room cool, never wobbled once. But I sit in my recliner and look up at it, and my chest gets tight, and I can't help but think those screws are finally gonna snap and the whole heavy mess is gonna come crashing down right on my head. It's exhausting, doc. I can't even trust a regular wooden kitchen chair to hold my weight without wondering if the legs are gonna splinter out from under me. I know a kitchen chair ain't a steel beam, I know my house ain't a construction site, but my brain just won't listen to reason anymore. It just keeps telling me that nothing is safe, nowhere, no how.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, do you often feel numb or detached from people, activities, or places that used to matter to you?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, \"detached\" is a mighty good word for it. It’s like... well, let me put it to you this way. My wife, she loves to host a card game. We’ve had this regular group coming over to the house for years now. She sets out the good bowls, puts out some mixed nuts, maybe makes a pot of decaf coffee so we ain't all up all night. And it used to be the highlight of my week, it really did. I'd look forward to it all week long. \n\nBut lately... I don't know. Just this past weekend, they were all over at the house. My wife will be sitting right there, just across the table from me, and the bridge fellows will be joking around, laughing about something in the newspaper or teasing each other about a bad hand they played. And they're loud, too, you know how a bunch of old guys get when they get together and get comfortable. \n\nBut me? I'm sitting right in the middle of it all, and I just feel completely hollowed out. My wife will be sitting right there and the bridge fellows will be joking around, but I feel like I'm behind a window and not really with them. I really do. It's like I'm looking through a thick pane of glass at my own kitchen, just watching them all move their mouths. I can hear the laughing, I can see my wife smiling, but it just doesn't touch me inside. I don't feel the joy of it, I don't feel the fellowship. I just sit there holding my cards, feeling a million miles away. \n\nI try to nod along, you know, so I don't ruin the evening for everybody else. I don't want my wife worrying over me more than she already does. But the truth of it is, I'm just waiting for them to pack up and go home. It’s a crying shame, doc, because they’re good fellas, salt of the earth types, but I just can't seem to join in no more. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doc, it ain't exactly that I stopped doing everything. I mean, a man's gotta have something to occupy his time, especially when he's not punching a clock no more and just sitting around the house all day. Take my garden out back, for instance. I got a little patch out there, mostly tomatoes—Beefsteaks and Early Girls, mostly—and some cucumbers and a few bell peppers. I still go out there every morning with the hose. I still water the garden, make sure the soil ain't drying out, pull a weed or two if I see 'em poking up through the mulch. 

But... well, it used to be I'd stand out there in the morning sun, smelling that wet dirt, and I'd just feel... I don't know, peaceful. Content, I guess. I'd get a real good feeling in my chest just watching things grow, knowing I was taking care of 'em. But now? Now I just stand there holding the nozzle, watching the water soak into the dirt, and... nothing. It's just water hitting dirt. The good feeling I used to get from it just doesn't come like it did. It just feels like another chore I gotta get through before I can go back inside and sit down.

And it's the exact same story with my radios. I don't know if I mentioned it before, but I've always liked messing around with electronics. Tinkering with old radios, specifically. The real old ones, you know, the heavy ones with the vacuum tubes and the big wooden cabinets that take up half a table. I got a couple of 'em sitting on the dining room table right now, actually, since I don't go out to the workbench in the garage much anymore. I'll sit there with my soldering iron, replacing a capacitor or trying to get the static to clear up so I can pull a station in from out of state. 

Used to be, when I'd finally hear that music come through the speaker, clear as a bell after hours of messing with the wires, I'd get this rush. A real thrill, like I'd brought something dead back to life. I still tinker with old radios some, I really do... but that thrill is just gone, doc. I fix a connection, the radio plays, the dial lights up, and I just feel flat. It’s like the color’s been washed out of everything. I go through the motions of doing the things I always liked doing, but the joy of it... the joy just packed up and left, I reckon. It makes it mighty hard to look forward to much of anything when even your hobbies feel like just staring at a blank wall.

My wife will be sitting right there and the bridge fellows will be joking around, but I feel like I'm behind a window and not really with them. I really do. It's like I'm looking through a thick pane of glass at my own kitchen, just watching them all move their mouths. I can hear the laughing, I can see my wife smiling, but it just doesn't touch me inside. I don't feel the joy of it, I don't feel the fellowship. I just sit there holding my cards, feeling a million miles away. \n\nI try to nod along, you know, so I don't ruin the evening for everybody else. I don't want my wife worrying over me more than she already does. But the truth of it is, I'm just waiting for them to pack up and go home. It’s a crying shame, doc, because they’re good fellas, salt of the earth types, but I just can't seem to join in no more. I'm just stuck on the other side of that glass.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you found yourself losing interest in things you used to enjoy, or feeling hopeless about the future?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, it ain't exactly that I stopped doing everything. I mean, a man's gotta have something to occupy his time, especially when he's not punching a clock no more and just sitting around the house all day. Take my garden out back, for instance. I got a little patch out there, mostly tomatoes—Beefsteaks and Early Girls, mostly—and some cucumbers and a few bell peppers. I still go out there every morning with the hose. I still water the garden, make sure the soil ain't drying out, pull a weed or two if I see 'em poking up through the mulch. \n\nBut... well, it used to be I'd stand out there in the morning sun, smelling that wet dirt, and I'd just feel... I don't know, peaceful. Content, I guess. I'd get a real good feeling in my chest just watching things grow, knowing I was taking care of 'em. But now? Now I just stand there holding the nozzle, watching the water soak into the dirt, and... nothing. It's just water hitting dirt. The good feeling I used to get from it just doesn't come like it did. It just feels like another chore I gotta get through before I can go back inside and sit down.\n\nAnd it's the exact same story with my radios. I don't know if I mentioned it before, but I've always liked messing around with electronics. Tinkering with old radios, specifically. The real old ones, you know, the heavy ones with the vacuum tubes and the big wooden cabinets that take up half a table. I got a couple of 'em sitting on the dining room table right now, actually, since I don't go out to the workbench in the garage much anymore. I'll sit there with my soldering iron, replacing a capacitor or trying to get the static to clear up so I can pull a station in from out of state. \n\nUsed to be, when I'd finally hear that music come through the speaker, clear as a bell after hours of messing with the wires, I'd get this rush. Mixed it right there in the yard, smoothed it out perfect. Solid as a rock. My wife bought that at Sears twenty years ago. It's been spinning up there all this time, keeping the room cool, never wobbled once. I got a little patch out there, mostly tomatoes—Beefsteaks and Early Girls, mostly—and some cucumbers and a few bell peppers. I still go out there every morning with the hose. The real old ones, you know, the heavy ones with the vacuum tubes and the big wooden cabinets that take up half a table. I got a couple of 'em sitting on the dining room table right now, actually, since I don't go out to the workbench in the garage much anymore. I'll sit there with my soldering iron, replacing a capacitor or trying to get the static to clear up so I can pull a station in from out of state. \n\nUsed to be, when I'd finally hear that music come through the speaker, clear as a bell after hours of messing with the wires, I'd get this rush. A real thrill, like I'd brought something dead back to life. I still tinker with old radios some, I really do... but that thrill is just gone, doc. I fix a connection, the radio plays, the dial lights up, and I just feel flat. It’s like the color’s been washed out of everything. I go through the motions of doing the things I always liked doing, but the joy of it... the joy just packed up and left, I reckon. It makes it mighty hard to look forward to much of anything when even your hobbies feel like just staring at a blank wall."
